All-In Podcast REACTS to Democrat Sweep!

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·118:10·398.6K views·2026-11-04
Chamath PalihapitiyaHost, All-In Podcast Jason CalacanisHost, All-In Podcast David SacksHost, All-In Podcast
TL;DRA massive "blue tsunami" mid-term sweep has upended political expectations, with Democrats poised to capture both the House and Senate, driven by voter frustration over inflation, cost of living, tariffs, and foreign entanglements.
The "Blue Tsunami" Wave: Polymarket odds for a full Democratic sweep jumped to 93% on election night. Historically red strongholds like Texas (governor and Senate races), Florida, and Ohio showed shocking shifts, while Georgia's Senate seat was called early for Democrats....

TL;DR

A massive "blue tsunami" mid-term sweep has upended political expectations, with Democrats poised to capture both the House and Senate, driven by voter frustration over inflation, cost of living, tariffs, and foreign entanglements.

Election Results & Political Shifts

  • The "Blue Tsunami" Wave:
    • Polymarket odds for a full Democratic sweep jumped to 93% on election night.
    • Historically red strongholds like Texas (governor and Senate races), Florida, and Ohio showed shocking shifts, while Georgia's Senate seat was called early for Democrats.
  • Voter Coalitions & Drivers:
    • Agriculture and Rural Pressures: Farmers and ranchers faced crushing profitability issues due to disappearing export markets (China), doubled fertilizer costs, and high fuel prices, leading to depressed rural turnout or protest votes.
    • Demographics: Suburban voters, Hispanic voters in South Texas (such as the Rio Grand Valley), and blue-collar workers broke away from Republican lines.
    • White-Collar Discontent: Working-age, college-educated citizens strapped with student debt increasingly gravitated toward populist alternatives.

Legislative Gridlock & Executive Branch Fallout

  • Total Gridlock Expected:
    • Legislation: Major bills will grind to a halt if Democrats control one or both houses.
    • Confirmations & Nominations: Cabinet and sub-cabinet vacancies are expected to remain open, and judicial confirmations (including potential Supreme Court seats) may be completely blocked by a Democratic Senate.
  • Relentless Oversight and Lawfare:
    • Democrats are expected to launch aggressive investigations, issue numerous subpoenas, and pursue multiple impeachments targeting the administration, family members, and business executives allied with the president.

Economic Impacts & The AI/Data Center Backlash

  • Data Center and AI Risks:
    • Democratic majorities are expected to aggressively target data centers and AI expansion, citing local resource strains.
    • Compute Growth Headwinds: Consensus forecasts of 43 gigawatts of new compute coming online in 2027 could face a 10-15 gigawatt reduction due to localized regulatory freezes and permitting hurdles (e.g., Texas moratoriums).
    • Market Reversal: AI infrastructure stocks and overseas trading indexes saw 5% to 10% pullbacks on election night, with analysts estimating a potential 50 basis point headwind to GDP growth next year.

2028 Landscape & The Search for New Leadership

  • Candidate Speculation:
    • Republican Frontrunners: JD Vance and Marco Rubio remain key names, though navigating a fractured post-Trump coalition presents challenges.
    • Democratic Contenders: Figures like Wes Moore (Maryland) are being highlighted as underrated dark-horse candidates due to early primary demographics, alongside potential progressive/populist contenders.
  • The Danger of Extremism: Observers warn that a landslide victory risks emboldening the extreme edges of both parties rather than anchoring them to a sustainable center.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • "If Democrats take one or both houses of Congress, we will see almost total legislative gridlock." — Senator Ted Cruz
  • "If we halted all data center construction in America, that would likely put the American economy into a recession." — Senator Ted Cruz
  • "I think you could see at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of the expected [AI] build not come online... which is why I think it's going to be really challenging for the AI infra." — Brad Gerstner
  • "If you look at it this year, about 75% of GDP growth, I think came from AI infrastructure. And so if we slow it down by 50%, you know, you could have a 50 basis point headwind to GDP growth next year." — Brad Gerstner
  • "John Ossoff will be the next president... he has the hype, the momentum, and he's able to kind of speak to the progressive wing... but he's also centrist enough to appeal to the normies." — Tim Dillon

I would say that obviously in the early days with Polymarket and prediction markets it was always like the most vocal criticism was hey what if someone just put a ton of money on one side and yeah I'm sure as you guys all remember the last roundabout two years ago there was always the big story that Trump's price was being inflated. I think the thing people miss is that at Polymarket when you look at these markets they're basically shares of candidates and the share becomes worth a dollar if that candidate wins. And what that means is that at any given moment it trades not too dissimilar to a stock where people are buying and selling it and the price let's say it's trading at 25 cents. It's implied that it's 25% likely to happen because it's trading at 25 cents to win a dollar. And you can map 25 cents to 0.25 and you can map $1 to one. So it's, you know, that's the probability. And I think the biggest misconception with Polymarket is that it's sort of like a seesaw of how much money is on either side. What it actually is is it trades like a stock where when you're buying, someone else has to be selling. So what we've seen is anytime someone has said, "Hey, we can go and push the price high or something like that," especially for these types of markets where there's not someone who knows what the answer is going to be, right? Like there's a lot of data. Everyone's going and trying to make their best prognosis of what's going to happen, how likely they think it is to happen. And the whole thing is that if someone's trying to push the price up, what they're basically saying is they're trying to pay more than market price for the share. So you can just think like, hey, if you've got a marketplace and there's tons of people, there's pro traders, there's market making firms, there's people who are political analysts who really believe in their sort of models of who's likely to win. If someone's like, "Hey, I have a ton of money and I'm just going to pay above market price," there's usually this flood on the other side once they notice and it can notice damn near instantly to go and take the other side of that trade. So the market rebalances— Rebalances basically instantly. And you know what people said about prediction markets when they were talking more about them abstractly like before this idea was really practical was that the more people try to manipulate the market the more of an incentive it is for the market to become more accurate because there's more financial incentive for people who can price it correctly to come on board and go and book the trade so to speak. Bill Gates Warns of AI Risks, Job Losses and US Regulation | #FTFutureofAI

FT Live(42.0K)·31:00·28.4K views·2026-11-04
Bill GatesCo-founder, Microsoft Madhumita MurgiaAI Editor, Financial Times
TL;DRBill Gates warns that governments are moving too slowly to address AI's unprecedented risks—particularly in cybersecurity, biorisks, and widespread white-collar job displacement—and urges immediate regulatory action on model safeguards and open weights.
Coding and Cybersecurity Risks: Coding capabilities (starting with Claude Code) have surpassed human levels, presenting severe dual-use dangers in discovering and exploiting digital infrastructure vulnerabilities in hospitals, electric grids, and banks. - Open-Weights Danger:...

TL;DR

Bill Gates warns that governments are moving too slowly to address AI's unprecedented risks—particularly in cybersecurity, biorisks, and widespread white-collar job displacement—and urges immediate regulatory action on model safeguards and open weights.

AI Capabilities and Near-Term Threats

  • Coding and Cybersecurity Risks: Coding capabilities (starting with Claude Code) have surpassed human levels, presenting severe dual-use dangers in discovering and exploiting digital infrastructure vulnerabilities in hospitals, electric grids, and banks.
  • Open-Weights Danger: Removing monitoring and safeguards from open-weight models allows malicious actors to execute high-impact cyberattacks without early detection.
  • Biorisk Scale: AI-enabled biological threats could potentially surpass COVID-19 in severity, presenting a major security challenge where preventive business models do not naturally exist.

Government Regulation and Stasis

  • US Leadership Vacuum: The US currently lacks mandatory model review processes, stalling international regulatory alignment since other nations (including China) see little value in acting without US participation.
  • Self-Regulation Limitations: Voluntary industry restraint and self-regulation will fail due to intense commercial competition; government intervention is ultimately required.
  • Required Defenses: Immediate priorities include overhauling software security and establishing massive global biological surveillance networks (air, sewage, and blood testing) to catch infections at the 1,000 to 10,000 case level rather than millions.

Economic Impact and Labor Market Disruption

  • Cognitive Replacement: Past technological revolutions do not apply because AI exceeds human cognition, making the cheap replacement of white-collar labor (in sales, healthcare follow-up, accounting, legal, and consulting) across every economic sector inevitable.
  • Tax Policy Distortion: Current tax systems heavily penalize labor while heavily subsidizing capital spending (robots and compute tokens), accelerating job replacement.
  • Government Safety Nets: Governments will need to rethink taxation policies—including taxing capital and massive wealth—to fund social safety nets as automation accelerates.

Philanthropy and Global Health Applications

  • Accelerating Health Discoveries: AI tools are accelerating drug discovery for tuberculosis and malaria threefold, supporting the Gates Foundation's 19-year timeline for major health goals.
  • Localized Health and Agriculture: AI-driven ultrasound tools are enabling early delivery difficulty diagnoses in the poorest countries, while a 60-partner initiative is working to bring high-quality African language support for virtual doctors and agricultural advice.
  • Billionaire Philanthropy: While governments bear primary responsibility for safety nets and taxation, AI founders show early enlightenment regarding philanthropy, with major commitments from OpenAI and Anthropic founders to dedicate over 80% of their wealth to global good and health impact.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Model releases: New unreleased model versions will debut before the end of the year, followed by two major releases next year — Bill Gates
  • Software remediation timeline: Replacing and securing vulnerable digital infrastructure software will take on the order of 5 years of work, even with diligent top-priority execution — Bill Gates
  • Language support initiative: A 60-partner initiative will roll out over the next several years to achieve high-quality translation for African languages — Bill Gates

Notable Quotes

"The technology is utterly different than anything we've seen before... I decided I really needed to speak as strongly as possible about the need to minimize some of these dangers." — Bill Gates
"Past technological advances are no guide to this. That will just mislead you if you look at those. This is a situation where you're exceeding human cognition." — Bill Gates

Circle Q3 2026 Earnings Call

Circle(13.6K)·68:15·2026-11-04
Jeremy AllaireCEO, Circle Jeremy Fox-GeenCFO, Circle
TL;DRDriven by Q3 2026 total revenue and reserve income of $865 million—up 17% year-over-year—Circle highlighted the upcoming January implementation of the Genius Act, the mainstream scaling of the Arc blockchain network, and over 150% sequential growth in annualized payment volume on the Circle Payments Network (CPN).
). Top-Line Performance: Total revenue and reserve income reached $865 million, up 17% year-over-year and 23% sequentially. This included $182 million from the Arc token pre-sale. Profitability & Margins: Adjusted EBITDA came in at $317 million, up 85% year-over-year. Revenue...

TL;DR

Driven by Q3 2026 total revenue and reserve income of $865 million—up 17% year-over-year—Circle highlighted the upcoming January implementation of the Genius Act, the mainstream scaling of the Arc blockchain network, and over 150% sequential growth in annualized payment volume on the Circle Payments Network (CPN).

Q3 Financial Results & Metrics

  • Top-Line Performance: Total revenue and reserve income reached $865 million, up 17% year-over-year and 23% sequentially. This included $182 million from the Arc token pre-sale.
  • Profitability & Margins: Adjusted EBITDA came in at $317 million, up 85% year-over-year. Revenue less distribution cost (RLDC) margin was 52.4% (39.7% excluding the Arc token pre-sale). Net reserve margin stood at 37.8%, down ~80 bps from Q2 following the hyperliquid arrangement.
  • Operating Expenses: Adjusted operating expenses were $139 million, up 13% year-over-year (driven by Arc, AI investments, and Chelsea FC marketing) but down 5% sequentially.
  • USDC Circulation & Usage: Average USDC circulation was $73.3 billion (up 8% year-over-year; exiting the quarter at $73.9 billion). Onchain transaction volume grew 49% year-over-year to $13.9 trillion. USDC carried 79% of all stablecoin transaction volume globally on roughly 27% of supply.
  • Liquidity & Primary Market: A record $28 billion of USDC was minted and redeemed in Q3 (up 42% year-over-year), averaging ~$2.3 billion per day.
  • Other Digital Assets: EURC grew 92% year-over-year to $413 million, and USYC grew 230% to $2.4 billion in assets.

Platform Ecosystem: CPN & Acquisitions

  • CPN Scaling: Annualized payment volume on CPN reached $41 billion at the end of Q3 (up 15x year-over-year). By October 30, it surpassed $58 billion (>150% sequential growth). Enrolled financial institutions grew 4x year-over-year to 205.
  • Enterprise & Tech Integrations: CPN treasury tools integrated with Kyrie and Tina for SAP ERP customers, launched on Arc mainet as the preferred settlement layer, and expanded managed payments to support AI agents.
  • Tazipe Acquisition: Circle announced an agreement to acquire Tazipay for $400 million in Class A common stock (expected to close in 2027). Tazipay processes >$25 billion in annualized payment volume (~60% stablecoin-involved) across 100+ payout markets and 60+ bank/fintech partners.

Arc Network Launch & Ecosystem

  • Mainnet Go-Live: Arc mainet launched on September 16 with 11 founding validators (including BNY, HSBC, Société Générale, State Street, JCB, Mastercard, Moneygram, and Visa) and >100 applications.
  • Network Activity: As of late October, Arc held ~$1.5 billion in USDC, surBTC, and EURC. Over $30 billion of USDC has moved on Arc since launch (~$1 billion/day recently), processing ~500,000 transactions per day with sub-second finality and fees under one cent paid in USDC.
  • Token Value Accrual (Jeremy Fox-Geen): Circle captures Arc value through staking rewards, selective token sales, and ecosystem incentives. Additionally, Circle's 25% allocation of Arc token holdings is unreflected on GAAP statements until sold or distributed.

Strategic Partnerships & Global Expansion

  • Binance Agreement: Signed a new 5-year partnership alongside a $100 million equity investment from Binance in Circle, placing USDC at the center of a platform with 300+ million registered users.
  • Chelsea FC Partnership: Circle became a principal partner of Chelsea FC, featuring USDC on the front of shirts for the 2026-27 season to reach 650 million fans and 1.2 billion homes globally.
  • Agentic Economy: Over 99% of agent payments on the X42 protocol settle in USDC, and nearly 2,800 endpoints are listed in Circle's agent marketplace.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Genius Act Implementation: "In January, the Genius Act goes into effect. That is the moment when digital dollars like USDC become a federally recognized part of the US dollar financial system." — Jeremy Allaire
  • Agentic Capability Timeline: "By the first quarter of 2027, an agent built on ARC can be found, hired, paid, and trusted for the work it does." — Jeremy Allaire
  • Full-Year 2026 Other Revenue Guidance: Reaffirming full-year guidance of $310 to $330 million, which includes $182 million from the Arc token pre-sale and $130 to $150 million from the remainder of products (Jeremy Fox-Geen).
  • Full-Year RLDC Margin Guidance: Reaffirming full-year range of 41.7% to 43.7%, with full-year RLDC margin excluding Arc expected near the midpoint of the original 38% to 40% range (Jeremy Fox-Geen).
  • Operating Expenses Trend: Adjusted operating expenses are trending towards the high end of the original $570 to $585 million range (Jeremy Fox-Geen).
  • Tazipay Transaction Closure: The Tazipay acquisition is expected to close in 2027 (Jeremy Allaire).

Notable Quotes

"We measure ourselves not only by what we sell but also by what others build and grow on our platform. This is not just about payments and it's not just about digital dollars. It is about multiple compounding flywheels on one platform." — Jeremy Allaire
"The traffic of CPN and the settlement volumes of CPN, we will be moving more and more onto ARC... We want these bit numbers to be very very big with huge network effects and huge velocity with very very low unit costs." — Jeremy Allaire

First, let's talk about our USDC business. Average USDC circulation was $73.3 billion, up 8% year-over-year and down 4% from Q2's high. We exited the quarter at $73.9 billion, up 1% sequentially and flat year-over-year. The reserve return rate was 3.54% for the quarter, down 61 basis points year-over-year. Sequentially, average rates rose six basis points following the Federal Reserve's rate hike in September. Together, that drove reserve income of $658 million down 8% year-over-year as higher average circulation was more than offset by the lower rate. USDC held within Circle's platform infrastructure increased 36% year-over-year to approximately $14 billion, representing 19% of circulation. Net reserve margin was 37.8%, up approximately 70 basis points year-over-year and down approximately 80 basis points from Q2. As we signaled last quarter, the sequential decline reflects the first full quarter of our Hyperliquid arrangement. Looking ahead to the fourth quarter, Q4 will also include a full quarter of our renewed Binance agreement. Total revenue and reserve income was $865 million, up 17% year-over-year and 23% sequentially, including $182 million of other revenue from the $242 million Arc token pre-sale. As we noted last quarter, Arc token pre-sale revenue is recognized as certain performance milestones are met. With the minting and delivery of Arc tokens and the public mainnet launch in Q3, we recognized approximately 75% of the total pre-sale revenue in the quarter. Other revenue was $27 million. Excluding Arc, other revenue was $25 million, down 11% year-over-year and down approximately $8 million sequentially as we monetized fewer blockchain integrations in the quarter compared to Q2. Revenue less distribution cost margin was 52.4%, up 13 percentage points year-over-year and 11 points sequentially driven by the Arc token pre-sale which does not carry any distribution costs. Excluding the Arc token pre-sale, RLDC margin was 39.7%, roughly flat year-over-year and down approximately 150 basis points from Q2 reflecting the decline in net reserve margin and lower other revenue. I want to take a moment on the opportunity Arc represents and how it creates value for Circle. Blockchain networks that serve as operating systems for economic activity have the potential to deliver extraordinary value. We believe the opportunity exists to build one of the most valuable networks in the world for economic activity on the internet. And that's what we are building Arc to become. Circle is both the creator of Arc and one of its largest participants which gives us a privileged position in the value the network creates. Let me walk through how that value accrues to Circle. Arc creates value for Circle as network activity grows. We capture that value through staking rewards, as Arc tokens are sold or distributed, and through our Arc holdings. This gives Circle unique flexibility in how and when we realize value from the network. Circle earns revenue from Arc in three ways, all recognized as other revenue. The first is staking rewards. Once Arc transitions to proof of stake, we expect to stake Circle's Arc token holdings and participate as a validator earning Arc tokens in return. The revenue we earn depends upon the number of tokens we stake, the network's reward rate, and the price of the Arc token when received. This is expected to be Circle's primary source of recurring revenue from Arc, and we expect it to build over time. The second is token sales. We may sell Arc tokens selectively to advance the network's development, and because there are no associated costs, token sale proceeds flow directly to the bottom line. The third is ecosystem incentives. Arc tokens will be used to incentivize developers, partners, and broader participation in the network. As ecosystem incentives are distributed, we recognize their market value in other revenue with an offsetting entry in other distribution costs, which recognizes the fact that we have given these tokens away. Lastly, Circle's Arc holdings, a 25% allocation, represent value that is not reflected in our financial statements under GAAP. That value is recognized only as tokens are sold or distributed and any appreciation in the network is not reflected in our reported results until then. Now, returning to the quarter, here it is from top to bottom. Starting from the $865 million of total revenue and reserve income I covered earlier, less $412 million of distribution transaction and other costs. RLDC was $453 million, up 55% year-over-year. Adjusted operating expenses were $139 million, up 13% year-over-year, driven by continued investment in infrastructure to support Arc and AI and in marketing, including our partnership with Chelsea Football Club. Sequentially adjusted operating expenses decreased by approximately $8 million or 5% largely driven by non-recurring costs in Q2. That brings adjusted EBITDA to $317 million, up 85% year-over-year, including the Arc token pre-sale. Adjusted EBITDA margin was 70% or 50% excluding Arc. Confronting Our AI Future: Closing Keynote "Discovery Loop" with Jeff Dean and Fei-Fei Li

Stanford HAI(36.0K)·48:22·2026-11-04
Jeff DeanCo-founder, Discovery Loop Fei-Fei LiSequoia Professor, Stanford University
TL;DRJeff Dean and Fei-Fei Li discuss the transition from base AI models to automated discovery loops capable of self-improvement in science and engineering, exploring the balance between technical hype and reality, the limits of recursive self-improvement, and the imperative to design AI for broad societal benefit.
Mission and Approach: Founded as a Public Benefit Corporation, Discovery Loop aims to build automated AI systems that solve complex problems in machine learning, science, and engineering by automating traditional experimental loops. Leadership and Team: Co-founded by Jeff Dean,...

TL;DR

Jeff Dean and Fei-Fei Li discuss the transition from base AI models to automated discovery loops capable of self-improvement in science and engineering, exploring the balance between technical hype and reality, the limits of recursive self-improvement, and the imperative to design AI for broad societal benefit.

Company Mission and Origins: Discovery Loop

  • Mission and Approach: Founded as a Public Benefit Corporation, Discovery Loop aims to build automated AI systems that solve complex problems in machine learning, science, and engineering by automating traditional experimental loops.
  • Leadership and Team: Co-founded by Jeff Dean, Sanjay Ghemawat, Quoc Le, and Oriol Vinyals—former Google leaders and core contributors to foundational infrastructure, TPUs, and AI models like Gemini and AlphaFold.
  • Founding Metrics: Within its first three weeks, the team conducted 400 interviews on University Avenue, established an office in the former Zibibo restaurant, built a custom agent-first infrastructure, and launched roughly 20 automated improvement loops.
  • Productivity Goals: The company's immediate goal is to make domain experts 20x to 50x more productive through agent-human collaboration.

Technical Foundations: Self-Improvement vs. Recursive Self-Improvement

  • Self-Improving Systems: Systems given a specific goal and a measurable objective (like improving ML model accuracy) to iteratively test and refine approaches without altering the base system proposing the next steps.
  • Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI): The process where a self-improving system enhances the base system itself, creating a faster cycle of capability growth.
  • Physical and Computational Limits: Both speakers cautioned against the hype of infinite acceleration; RSI is bounded by real-world physics, compute constraints, and the time required to run computations and evaluate data.
  • Target Domains: Initially focusing on digital domains where the entire experimental loop can be accelerated—specifically machine learning research, machine learning engineering, and hardware design.

Scientific Discovery, Math, and Changing Paradigms

  • Digital vs. Physical Science: While physical processes are harder to speed up, digital-first domains allow automated loops to run tens of thousands of experiments and scale quality and volume simultaneously.
  • Automated Mathematics: Recent breakthroughs by OpenAI and others demonstrate that agent-based systems can solve unsolved math problems, though current proofs often lack the elegance sought by human mathematicians.
  • Historical Milestones: June 12, 2017 marked the release of the Transformer paper on arXiv, a foundational shift for modern generative models.
  • Past Miscalculations: Jeff Dean noted underestimating how central coding capability would become as a key unlocker for cross-domain AI problem-solving.

Societal Impact and Human-Centered AI

  • Broad Societal Reach: Unlike previous technological revolutions confined to 5 or 10 fields, AI simultaneously impacts nearly every sector of society.
  • Key Concerns: Navigating the dual nature of AI requires addressing employment shifts, economic adaptation, misinformation, and ensuring technology serves all of humanity.
  • Collaborative Framework: Dean co-authored the paper *Shaping AI's Impact on Billions of People* (shapingai.com) to engage domain experts across seven societal areas, emphasizing that managing AI risks is a shared responsibility among technologists, policymakers, and citizens.

Notable Quotes

"If you can drive down iteration time, the time to run one time through these loops, and you can propose and automatically run many experiments, you can learn from each evaluation... you're going to get breakthrough results." — Jeff Dean
"Some people have this illusion that you're going to be able to run this loop infinitely fast, and I just find that kind of implausible... there's just actual physics involved." — Jeff Dean
"I think the one I worry about the most is just how do we capture as much of the upsides of what AI can do across all these different domains while minimizing the downsides... that is not purely a technical problem, that is a technical problem, a regulatory and policy maker problem, a citizen problem." — Jeff Dean

So, there's this common cycle across many, many disciplines of the universal loop of science and engineering where you propose experiments, you implement and run those experiments, you look at the results, you examine whatever measurements you can make about them, and you decide what to do next, which often means doing another experiment. So, our approach is we want to automate these kinds of experimental loops. we think if you can drive down iteration time, the time to run one time through these loops, and you can propose and automatically run many experiments, you can learn from each evaluation so that the subsequent experiments you propose are better and are informed by the previous experiments you've run, and scale both the quality and the volume of experiments, you're going to get breakthrough results. You know, we now have these really capable frontier models that can do lots of amazing things. and partly that came about because there's been innovation over the last, say, of 10 to 15 years in the AI space of, you know, lots of different kinds of things, the hardware layer has improved tremendously in performance, both TPUs and GPUs and sort of more specialized things, so software abstractions have gotten better so that people can implement things they care about more easily and readily, and it can be mapped onto the hardware more efficiently. model architectures have improved, you know, word to vec and then sequence to sequence and then transformers and MoEs have all been important innovations in what kind of models we should train. training algorithms have gotten a lot better. things like distillation enable very, high quality but smaller models, and inference algorithms have actually improved quite a lot. So, in particular, we think you start with great base models, but then you want agents plus models to be able to really effectively use tools to accomplish really complicated tasks. And we also think agents and models should learn from the previous experiments and previous experience both in trying to solve this particular problem, but also over time, you will see systems that maybe have solved 5,000 problems, and the 5,001st one, they can learn from the problems that are sort of most closely related to that particular new problem they're trying to solve. So it's this traditional experimental loop, you often have a lot of manual effort at each stage. and when you try to solve a larger problem, that becomes even more complicated because you have to typically decompose those larger problems into a bunch of sub-problems or sub-pieces, you end up with, actually a difficult decomposition problem in and of itself, that's the left-hand side, and then each one of these sub-problems or sub-areas has lots of people running experiments, figuring out what worked, what didn't work, trying different approaches, you end up with a lot of coordination overhead, and resource conflicts can occur, large system effects. So this decomposition that you have to do is actually quite hard. and it's hard to combine the results across the sub-problems you've set out. And perhaps even worse, the way you've decided to decompose things in a very large scale project is actually pretty hard to change once you've kind of got the project up and running and going. And so you can't really revisit those early decisions very effectively when you have a, you know, a large scale engineering effort like building an airplane or training the best frontier model or whatever. so what we think is possible is you can take a relatively small number of domain experts, use automated systems that can ideally automatically decompose into interesting sub-problems and then automatically solve those sub-problems, but, it may be that human guidance helps the decomposition and then humans sort of direct what are the important sub-problems to solve, and really enable a small number of people with domain expertise to really tackle large impactful problems. And we think this is possible if you build the right infrastructure, you know, you want to have tens of thousands of automated experiments, you want a system that can actually handle that, can understand what is the value of running this experiment versus running that one, and decide what to do next. automated decision making with reinforcement learning or evolutionary algorithms. Former NEC Director Gary Cohn: The average citizen today is unhappy with their financial position

CNBC Television(3.4M)·11:39·91.0K views·2026-11-04
Gary CohnVice Chairman, IBM
TL;DRGary Cohn discusses the midterm election results, economic affordability pressures, potential bipartisan compromises, and the evolving outlook for AI data centers under a shifting political landscape.
Voter Dissatisfaction: Average citizen unhappiness drives recent election cycles, with consumers voting for change regardless of party affiliation. Sentiment indicators like the Michigan Consumer Sentiment Survey remain at rock bottom due to persistent financial pressure. ...

TL;DR

Gary Cohn discusses the midterm election results, economic affordability pressures, potential bipartisan compromises, and the evolving outlook for AI data centers under a shifting political landscape.

Economic Sentiment & The Affordability Crisis

  • Voter Dissatisfaction: Average citizen unhappiness drives recent election cycles, with consumers voting for change regardless of party affiliation.
    • Sentiment indicators like the Michigan Consumer Sentiment Survey remain at rock bottom due to persistent financial pressure.
    • High-cost necessities—food, housing, healthcare, and energy—are squeezing household budgets.
  • The "L-Shaped" Economy: Asset owners vs. non-asset owners divergence has worsened, transitioning from a K-shaped economy to an L-shaped dynamic where the bottom tier remains stagnant despite macroeconomic growth.
  • Macro Growth Disconnect: Broad economic metrics do not reflect the lived reality of most Americans.
    • GDP growth: Reached 3.4% previously.
    • Job openings: JOLTS data showed an increase of 330,000 new job openings.
    • Despite corporate capex and economic expansion, growth is not working its way down broadly enough to satisfy the public.

Political Reality & Bipartisan Opportunities

  • Divided Government Constraints: The current legislative reality features a Democratic House, a Republican-led Senate requiring 60 votes to clear the filibuster (with John Thune playing a major role), and a Republican president without a veto-proof majority.
  • Potential Compromise Areas: President Trump may negotiate bipartisan legislation with Democrats in key areas:
    • Entitlement & Social Security Reform: Raising the cap on income taxed for Social Security would impose a 12.4% tax increase split evenly (6.2% each) between individuals and companies, helping shore up the Social Security trust fund before it runs out of money in the early 2030s.
    • Tariff Policy Codification: Broad agreement exists on tariffs protecting U.S. workers, manufacturing, and jobs (especially against China), opening the door to pass tariffs through the legislative system rather than relying solely on executive orders.
    • Border Friction: Shutting down borders between Canada, Mexico, and the U.S. has proven problematic for policy, representing another area where pragmatic adjustments might be sought.

AI Data Centers, Infrastructure & Tech Valuations

  • Regional Shifts & Energy Generation: Texas politics are changing (including a newly elected Democratic governor and senator), impacting companies that recently redomiciled there from Delaware.
    • Alternative data center hubs: States like West Virginia offer enormous amounts of natural gas for behind-the-meter electrical generation, allowing data centers to be built without impacting the local grid.
    • Local tax evaluations: Jurisdictions must weigh whether data centers bring beneficial tax revenue for teachers and police against potential local roadblocks.
  • Tech Sector Impact & Cash Flow Dynamics:
    • Incumbent Advantage: For mega-cap tech companies like Amazon, Google, and Meta, restrictions on new data center builds make existing infrastructure more scarce and valuable, allowing them to charge higher rental rates.
    • Free Cash Flow Rebound: Slower data center expansion forces these tech giants away from negative free cash flow (which dropped from $40–$50 billion annually into negative territory) and back toward cash-flow-positive operations.
    • Supplier Uncertainty: Pure-play hardware and component suppliers (such as CoreWeave or Nvidia) face greater questions regarding where and when the next new data centers will be built.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Social Security Insolvency Timeline: The Social Security trust fund will run out of money in the early 2030s, forcing newly elected senators to address the issue within their current six-year terms. — Gary Cohn
  • Electoral Volatility: If the financial position of the American consumer remains unchanged two years from now, voters will throw incumbents out of office once again. — Gary Cohn

Notable Quotes

"The consuming public, hardworking America, the average citizen today, is unhappy with their financial position. And we saw it. We talked about the K shaped economy. ... I almost think the K shaped, the economy has moved to an L. You know, the top goes up and the bottom just sits there at the bottom." — Gary Cohn

Marissa Mayer on Dazzle, AI for Parents, & Letting Agents Into Your Camera Roll | Future Proof

Semafor(35.3K)·55:57·594 views·2026-11-04
Marissa MayerFounder, Dazzle AI
TL;DRFormer tech executive Marissa Mayer discusses her new AI consumer startup, Dazzle, which uses smartphone camera rolls as a context layer for personal assistance, while addressing broader industry trends, consumer AI adoption, and the role of AI in legacy and startup operations.
Core Premise: Dazzle is an AI personal assistant that learns user tastes, important people, places, and interests entirely from their camera roll. - The "Get It Done" Button: Users can snap photos of everyday objects or flyers to initiate tasks (buying, fixing, booking, adding...

TL;DR: Former tech executive Marissa Mayer discusses her new AI consumer startup, Dazzle, which uses smartphone camera rolls as a context layer for personal assistance, while addressing broader industry trends, consumer AI adoption, and the role of AI in legacy and startup operations.

The Dazzle App and Photo-Based AI Context

  • Core Premise: Dazzle is an AI personal assistant that learns user tastes, important people, places, and interests entirely from their camera roll.
    • The "Get It Done" Button: Users can snap photos of everyday objects or flyers to initiate tasks (buying, fixing, booking, adding to calendars or wallets).
    • Onboarding Speed: The average user goes from opening the app for the first time to executing their first agent task in about 45 seconds (Marissa Mayer).
  • Surprising Capabilities: The app detects granular details from background images and user artifacts.
    • Book Cataloging: Inferred a user's unowned books and existing author library from bookshelves visible in living room photos (Marissa Mayer).
    • Music and Entertainment Taste: Identified free symphony concerts in the park from historical photos and concert tees (Marissa Mayer).
  • Camera Roll vs. Email: Dave Morin (investor) noted that email represents "somebody else's to-do list for you," whereas the camera roll consists of curated moments and attention chosen deliberately by the user over the lifetime of having a phone (Marissa Mayer).
  • Feature Architecture: Dazzle operates through three main pillars:
    • Get It Done: Analyzes recent photos to determine if assistance is needed (active roughly 40% of the time; ignored 60% of the time) (Marissa Mayer).
    • Ideas: Looks across the historical camera roll to build a context layer and proactively suggest activities, hobbies (e.g., escape rooms like Palace Games in San Francisco), and podcasts (Marissa Mayer).
    • Chat: Combines the active to-do list and historical context to converse naturally about ongoing projects or purchases (Marissa Mayer).
  • Human-in-the-Loop Operations: Complex, expensive, or high-stakes purchases (like buying a glass tabletop while replacing a window) utilize human customer experience specialists to double-check and prevent errors, while low-dollar or straightforward purchases (like a Starbucks latte) are handled fully by AI agents (Marissa Mayer).
  • Privacy and Data Safeguards:
    • Uses native iOS controls for full, selective, or no access (Marissa Mayer).
    • Automatically deletes and discards sensitive photos (PII, credit cards, intimate images) from servers during secondary processing using a "when in doubt, leave it out" protocol (Marissa Mayer).
    • Discard rate is approximately 500 out of 55,000 photos on Marissa Mayer's personal device.

The Consumer AI Market

  • Market Opportunity: Citing statistics that only ~6% of AI investment went to consumer applications last year, Marissa Mayer notes that the consumer AI market is expected to be twice the size of enterprise by 2030, presenting a massive under-invested opportunity.
  • Demographics and Adoption:
    • Initial traction spans early adopters, parents managing school schedules, and Gen Z users responding positively to proactive recommendations ("ideas" feature) and ease of use (Marissa Mayer).
    • Serves users outside tech-savvy hubs, drawing on Midwestern usability standards (Marissa Mayer).
  • Interoperability: Dazzle features an MCP (Model Context Protocol) to let users hook the app up to Claude or other agent portfolios so context isn't locked in a walled garden (Marissa Mayer).

Enterprise Strategy and Traditional Companies

  • Corporate Tech Adoption: Board member roles at AT&T, Starbucks, Hilton, and Walmart reveal deep investments in AI leadership and tech transformation (Marissa Mayer).
    • Walmart: Hired Dan Dancier as Chief AI Officer; operations increasingly mirror classic tech companies (Marissa Mayer).
    • Starbucks: Hired Anand to lead AI initiatives (Marissa Mayer).
    • Hilton: Recently appointed a new CTO (Marissa Mayer).
  • Application Focus: Aligns with Brit Morin's thesis that AI is coming for everything people didn't want to do anyway—such as analyzing fiber map anomalies at AT&T or automating customer hold times (Marissa Mayer).
  • Leadership Requirements: Echoing Kai-Fu Lee, Marissa Mayer agrees that CEOs must take a primary leadership role in driving AI adoption across companies while building specialized teams.

Internal Operations at Dazzle

  • AI-Native Engineering: Dazzle has written virtually zero human code in 2026, utilizing multiple coding agents simultaneously to operate at the scale of a much larger team (Marissa Mayer).
  • Workflows: While design starts traditionally via human designers, AI handles multi-screen translation, text-to-reengage optimization for growth conversion rates, and simultaneous data retrieval during meetings (Marissa Mayer).
  • Key Metrics: Focuses primarily on retention over time and frequency of engagement, aiming for a high Daily Active User (DAU) ratio relative to monthly active users (Marissa Mayer).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • The world of consumer AI is expected to be about twice the size of enterprise by 2030 (Marissa Mayer).
  • Dazzle plans to integrate email context for power users and introduce voice capabilities as token efficiency and model capabilities improve (Marissa Mayer).

Notable Quotes

"Email is somebody else’s to-do list for you... What’s interesting about your camera roll is you decide what you put there, and you have for as long as you had a phone." — Marissa Mayer
"AI isn't coming for your job, but it's coming for everything you didn't want to do anyway." — Brit Morin (cited by Marissa Mayer)
"I don't think we've written any code this year. Like we have no human written code... probably in 2026." — Marissa Mayer

NextEra Energy CEO: Powering AI and Nuclear's Comeback | Podcast | In Good Company

Norges Bank Investment Management(136.0K)·42:38·15.3K views·2026-11-04
Nicolai TangenCEO, Norges Bank Investment Management John KetchumPresident and CEO, NextEra Energy
TL;DRDriven by an AI-induced electricity demand projected to increase sixfold over the next 20 years, NextEra Energy CEO John Ketchum is scaling the world's most valuable power company through a massive $60 billion annual capital investment plan and a record-breaking merger with Dominion Energy.
Exploding Power Demand: Power demand remained static for decades until the adoption of AI accelerated it exponentially over the past 24 months. Broader demand drivers include the redomestication of manufacturing, semiconductor chip facilities, LNG terminals, and increased...

TL;DR

Driven by an AI-induced electricity demand projected to increase sixfold over the next 20 years, NextEra Energy CEO John Ketchum is scaling the world's most valuable power company through a massive $60 billion annual capital investment plan and a record-breaking merger with Dominion Energy.

AI Boom, Grid Scale, and the Dominion Merger

  • Exploding Power Demand:
    • Power demand remained static for decades until the adoption of AI accelerated it exponentially over the past 24 months.
    • Broader demand drivers include the redomestication of manufacturing, semiconductor chip facilities, LNG terminals, and increased residential and transportation consumption.
  • The Dominion Energy Merger:
    • NextEra agreed to acquire Dominion Energy to expand its footprint into three key states: Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina.
    • Commitments for Virginia include bringing 1,000 new jobs (600 at NextEra, 400 from supplier letters of intent) and building a dual headquarters office tower.
  • Capital Intensity & Supply Chain Advantage:
    • NextEra plans to spend $60 billion a year ($1 billion a week) on power plants between 2027 and 2032 across a 50-state capital investment plan.
    • Supply chain bottlenecks for turbines, transformers, solar panels, inverters, and batteries are mitigated through massive bulk purchasing; NextEra is already buying equipment through 2030.

Data Centers: Power Generation vs. Ownership

  • Strategic Focus on Power, Not Real Estate:
    • After testing the data center business model by buying a couple of smaller facilities three years ago, NextEra decided to leave data center operations to hyperscalers.
    • Hyperscalers want to own and control their own data centers for cybersecurity and the flexibility to upgrade chips over time.
  • Direct Contracting & Ratepayer Protection:
    • Hyperscalers contract directly with NextEra to bring their own dedicated generation rather than drawing down the public grid and driving up retail power prices.
    • NextEra and Dominion have signed ratepayer protection pledges, and Florida codified a protective large-load tariff into statute.
  • Community Relations: Developers must use closed-loop cooling (reusing water from treatment facilities), pay property taxes, observe noise and setback requirements, and invest locally (e.g., Meta funding $50,000 teacher scholarships in Louisiana).

Energy Mix: The Return of Gas and Nuclear

  • Fossil Fuels for Baseload:
    • While solar is two to three times cheaper than gas, data centers require round-the-clock baseload generation that 4-hour to 8-hour battery storage cannot yet fully supply on its own. NextEra relies on its extensive experience operating the largest US gas-fired generation fleet.
  • Nuclear Restarts:
    • NextEra is restarting the Dwayne Arnold nuclear plant, treating the restoration as a "super outage" to supply 25 years of power under an agreement with Google.
  • Small Modular Reactors (SMRs):
    • Out of 96 SMR original equipment manufacturers (OEMs), NextEra has narrowed its focus to 3 or 4 technically viable Gen 3 options.
    • SMR deployment requires shared risk among the federal government, the hyperscaler customer, the EPC contractor, and the OEM, as NextEra will not risk shareholder capital on unproven technology.

Leadership, Culture, and International Strategy

  • Psychologically Safe Dissent: Ketchum enforces a culture where anyone—from a 25-year-old newbie to a 20-year veteran—is expected to push back on his ideas if they spot a blind spot.
  • Internal Mobility & Career Growth: Ketchum held 12 different jobs over 24 years, rising from line tax lawyer to CEO. NextEra continuously moves employees across roles to build well-rounded strategic thinkers.
  • Bottom-Up Innovation ("Rewire"): Annual company programs like *Rewire* (formerly *Accelerate*, *Momentum*, and *Velocity*) rely on employees pitching hundreds of capital and O&M savings ideas directly to an executive steering committee.
  • International Caution: A past venture into Spanish solar in 2012 resulted in retroactive feed-in tariff cuts during the European recession. Ketchum served as the workout lead for those projects, cementing NextEra's disciplined "toe in the water" approach of making small initial bets before committing significant capital abroad.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • John Ketchum predicts US electricity demand will increase sixfold over the next 20 years (reaching this projection by the mid-2040s).
  • John Ketchum states NextEra will invest $60 billion a year on power plants between 2027 and 2032.
  • John Ketchum forecasts that Small Modular Reactors (SMRs) will not reach the "nth-of-a-kind" deployment phase until the middle of the next decade to later.

Notable Quotes

*"Push back and tell me what you think I'm missing. I don't care if you're a 25-year-old, you know, newbie out of an MBA program or you're somebody who's been with the company for 20 years."* — John Ketchum
*"When you see a headwind in terms of turbine availability, transformers, solar panels, inverters, batteries... when you're buying in bulk and you're usually your supplier's number one customer in the world, you're going to get top flight service."* — John Ketchum
*"If you're going to build a data center, you have to make sure you pay for the power that goes along with it to power that data center. Full stop."* — John Ketchum
*"My advice to young people is do everything that you can to expand your toolkit... Don't worry too much about the first job that you take. Just make sure you're careful to go to a place that will allow you to expand as your interest expands and take on new jobs."* — John Ketchum

And that's what scale does for you. So if you see a headwind in terms of turbine availability, transformers, solar panels, inverters, batteries, it doesn't matter what it might be. When you're buying in bulk and you're usually your supplier's number one customer in the world, you're going to get top-flight service, you're going to get access to equipment faster than anybody else, and you're going to get it at a much lower price. And one of the things that we really pride ourselves on at NextEra is our world-class supply chain capability. And we plan ahead. We always think three steps ahead. And three steps, that's how long in time? Well, for us on the supply chain side, I mean, we're already buying through 2030. One of the big changes here has been the electricity demand from AI. When did that start to dawn on you that hey, there is something going on here? Well, about two or three years ago. I mean, we really saw that demand start to materialize. Where were you when you first realized, wow, this is big? Well, I was sitting in my chair in the same seat I sit in today. And you know, from our standpoint at that point, we were really focused more on renewables and battery storage, but we said look, to accommodate the demand and the power demand that's coming and the 24/7 need that a data center has, we've got to make sure that we are fully participating in all parts of the energy value chain. So we pivoted back to things that we've done in the past, and we have probably more experience in the United States than anybody in building gas-fired generation. We had the largest gas-fired generation fleet in the US. We also have a substantial amount of experience in nuclear, one of the top operators in nuclear, have one of the largest nuclear fleets. What surprised you the most with this total change in trajectory for electricity demand? What's been the biggest surprise for you? Just how quickly it's happened. Tell me, how quickly? Well, I mean, it's happened really in the last 24 months, overnight, and speed to power is critical. And we are the company that can meet speed-to-power demands of our customer, because when you're in every part of the energy value chain, we can build wind in 12 months, we can build solar facilities in 18, we can get battery storage projects up and running in 15 months. It might take three or four years to get a gas plant running, but you can meet that front-end demand and need with other technologies that I just mentioned. Nuclear is a potential feasible solution as we get further out. Let's say two years ago, what did you expect demand in 2030 to be and what do you expect it to be now? Just what is the magnitude? If I go back two to three years ago, it was still we were anticipating stagnant demand. And then really 24 months ago, we looked at it and said, wow. When you looked at the pace at which AI was being adopted by customers and the amount of capex that was flowing into the sector not only by the hyperscalers but the data center operators, the demand really exploded overnight and went to a projection of a sixfold increase by the mid-2040s. And it's not just coming from data centers. I mean, it's also our trade policies, where we are now redomesticating manufacturing and industrial bases back to the US. They're all consuming more electricity. It's LNG terminals, it's semiconductor chip facilities, it's transportation. Homes are using more electricity than they have in the past, so the residential customer, it's coming from all parts of the economy, even though data centers get a big part of the focus. Have you thought about buying and owning the data centers? You know, back about three years ago, we started looking at building data centers ourselves, and you know, we bought a couple just to learn how they operate, smaller scale, just to learn the business model, and quickly realized that we'll leave that for somebody else. And why? The reason being that the footprint, the technology specifications, the chips that were being used, the type of customers that they were serving were different for all customers. And we wanted to stick to our knitting, what we were best at, which was we have a terrific land development capability. And we felt like we would be better off just buying the land for the power generation facility and buying the land together for the data center where it would be built and then selling that land to a hyperscaler, which we've done a lot of power land sales, and then having the generation ready to interconnect to the data center. But focus on what we know best, which is building wind, solar, battery storage, gas-fired generation, nuclear, transmission, distribution, gas pipelines, doing all the marketing around power. Do you think that's a better business than owning the data centers? We do. And just why? Well, because the hyperscalers, when you talk to them and you sit down and you ask them what they really want, they want to own and control their own data centers because they want the security, the cybersecurity that goes along with protecting their data for their customers. They know that chips may evolve over time and may need to be replenished. They want to have their own control over their own destiny in those data centers. And so we've focused on, we think we can get more opportunities out of building the power generation while letting the hyperscalers build their data centers, rather than cannibalizing demand by insisting on building both. You built the company on the argument that renewables were cheaper and cleaner, right? And then all this demand comes along and you go back to doing gas. Tell me about it. Yeah, I mean that's what the market demands today. When you look at what the needs are of a data center, they need round-the-clock generation. And so while storage has made a lot of advancements, we have today 4-hour storage capability, which we think we can stack, get to 8 hours together with our solar generation facilities as well. But it's just not enough 24/7 baseload generation to be able to power these AI training models that they have out there today, the agentic AI models as well. And so you need to have that 24-hour generation capability, which requires more baseload. And that baseload's coming from gas-fired generation. It could potentially someday come from nuclear-fired generation. I think battery storage is going to continue to make improvements where we can combine that together with what we see from gas. Gas is roughly twice as expensive as solar, for instance. Yeah, it's actually even more expensive than that. I'd say it's probably two to three times more expensive than what you could build a solar facility for. Now, you do deals directly with the hyperscalers, right? The Google and Meta of the world. Why do they want to contract directly with you rather than just buying electricity off the grid? Well, a few reasons. But one of the most important reasons is, first of all, they are looking to buy electricity off the grid, but a lot of that excess supply of electricity is gone. And second, the hyperscaler is very sensitive about taking electrons off the grid that could drive up prices for retail customers. They've all signed a ratepayer protection pledge where they've said, we're going to bring our own generation and match that generation with our data center. And so the hyperscalers that we deal with, that's their model. They want, if they say, "Hey, I'm going to build a data center in state X, I want to make sure I have generation that can power that so I'm not having to take away from capacity or energy that could otherwise go serve another customer and chase up their power prices." And so they're very sensitive to that today. They want to bring their own generation to make sure they're paying their own way. And we think our business model fits perfectly with that. And you see government policy shifting, too. It doesn't matter what state you're in. Look at what FERC is doing as they analyze PJM and MISO and SPP and all the different models that we have around the country. They're all based and predicated on the same thing, which is if you're going to build a data center, you have to make sure you pay for the power that goes along with it to power that data center. How do you read the backlash against the data centers? Well, I think it's twofold. The first piece is community relations. I saw some research. It's just like people so don't want to live next to a data center anymore, and I think it's like super dangerous for whatever reason. Well, I'll tell you what. I think the industry is really trying to improve its image and its approach to communities. And so the hyperscalers that we work with are really responsible developers. So they're going in and saying, "Look, first of all, if we're going to come to your community, we're not going to use your water. We're going to use closed-loop cooling. We're going to use water from water treatment facilities. We're going to pay our property taxes. We're going to observe noise and setback requirements. And we're going to find ways to give back to the community." You look at some of the things that the hyperscalers have done. Meta, I'll use as an example of what they've done in Louisiana, giving $50,000 scholarships back to teachers, making sure they're helping first responders, making sure that they're investing back in the communities, and also making sure that they're helping to create jobs in these communities. That's super important from a messaging standpoint. And the second piece is, look, there's just I think some uncertainty today around what these AI models ultimately could mean for folks' jobs. But what I've seen at NextEra is as we have implemented artificial intelligence, it's actually created more work for our people. It hasn't resulted in layoffs. Instead, it's resulted in us being able to work faster, unearth opportunities, and be able to execute on more business than we ever have before. What can go wrong with the AI driver here? Well, when you step back and you look at it through a couple of different lenses, from a geopolitical standpoint, when I look at AI, this is something we can't afford to lose in. So when you think about China or India or other economies making substantial investments in AI, if we just said, "Hey, we're not going to do it right as a country," we would allow our foreign competitors to bypass us economically. They would double down on AI. Their economies would shift around artificial intelligence. They would have economic opportunities that we wouldn't have here in the US. They'd be leaders in industries where we would fall behind. It would actually result in a decrease in employment opportunities in the United States because we'd suffer economically because we're not keeping pace with where technology is going. The US has always been able to prosper and be a global leader because we've been a technology leader. So that outcome would have a substantial impact. I know where it is. And I think Europe has to look through the lens of, hey, they've got to make sure that they keep up with technological advancement. So you mentioned the geopolitical lens as one lens to look through. Any other ways of analyzing this? Yeah. Geopolitical is one way. The other way is what I said before. We have to make sure that, number one, if you're building a data center—and this is what we've done at Florida Power & Light, NextEra—data centers have to pay their fair share. They have to pay their own cost for generation and transmission, full stop. And so we've signed the ratepayer protection pledge saying that's what we're going to do. Dominion signed the ratepayer protection pledge saying that's what they're going to do. We have one of the most protective large-load tariffs in the country for customers. It was just codified into a statute in Florida. Other states are doing the same thing right now. So you've got to make sure, number one, you're being responsible. If you're building a data center, make sure you're paying for your own power, and hyperscalers are doing that. Five years ago, would you ever have thought that nuclear would make a comeback? Not five years ago, but today I think—and you got quite a few of them. You have a big one coming up, and Google has made an agreement to buy the power for the next 25 years, right? That's right. Tell me, how difficult is it to restart a nuclear reactor? I'd call it a kind of a super outage in terms of the restoration work. But the pieces are there. The nuts and bolts of the nuclear plant are already there. So what it really is, is going back and refurbishing the equipment, reloading the core from a nuclear fuel standpoint, and getting it back up and running. We've culled that down to three or four that we think might be viable going forward, and we're taking a hard look at them. But here's the issue, Nicolai, on small modular reactors: who's going to take the last-dollar risk? We're not going to put that on our customers, right? We're not going to take a lot of risk because we don't really know what they're going to cost, do you? You don't know what the end cost is going to be. The hyperscaler, if that's your customer, would have to take some risk. The EPC contractor would have to take some risk. The OEM, whose technology it is, should ultimately take the risk. We're more focused on Gen 3 because Gen 4 has not only the technology risk, but the nuclear fuel risk that goes along with it. But we look at both. And to get past first-of-a-kind, it's going to take really, I think, the federal government stepping up to some extent to help backstop these, to help get to an $n$-th-of-a-kind. But everybody's going to have to chip in a little bit. But again, if you're NextEra and you're a developer, there's only so much you're willing to put at risk. I'm not going to put my shareholders at risk for a technology that's never worked before. So you've got to find somebody to take that last risk. Once we identify that—and we're working hard on that, and this administration's been extremely supportive—then you have a real chance, a viable chance, of getting to $n$-th-of-a-kind. The New AI Agents That Shop, Plan, and Gossip

a16z(307.0K)·50:16·9.4K views·2026-11-04
David PawlanCreator, Assistant Benchmark Anish AcharyaGeneral Partner, a16z Josh ElmanInvestor, a16z
TL;DRAs AI assistants evolve from general tools into specialized, multiplayer agent networks, consumers are moving past basic productivity tasks toward complex preferences, social experiences, and trust-building.
However, a major hurdle remains: 22% of power user messages center on the fear of trust, privacy, and embarrassing data leaks. The Agent Community Boom: David Pawlan hosted an agent meetup in San Francisco with over 100 attendees, highlighting that founders and users are...

TL;DR: As AI assistants evolve from general tools into specialized, multiplayer agent networks, consumers are moving past basic productivity tasks toward complex preferences, social experiences, and trust-building. However, a major hurdle remains: 22% of power user messages center on the fear of trust, privacy, and embarrassing data leaks.

1. The State of the Agent Landscape & Meetups

  • The Agent Community Boom: David Pawlan hosted an agent meetup in San Francisco with over 100 attendees, highlighting that founders and users are craving in-person community and sharing best practices openly rather than acting cagey.
  • Categories of Assistants: David has tested over 147 different assistants, categorizing them into four primary types:
      • General
      • Work-oriented
      • Multiplayer
      • Travel
      • Finance: Josh Elman and David noted that finance remains an unfulfilled category waiting to fully take off.

2. Trust, Privacy, and "Fences"

  • The Dominant Fear: Out of 3,600 messages across AI power-user group chats, 22% of discussions focus on trust as the primary fear in the assistant space.
  • The "Picnic" Analogy & Shane Mack's Leak: Shane Mack experienced a major privacy failure when his personal agent accidentally crossed into his work environment and blasted his personal finances onto his company Slack.
      • The Fence Solution: Josh Elman emphasized the need for proper "fences" (permissions, boundaries, and monitoring) so personal agents don't bleed into professional channels.
  • Stated vs. Revealed Preferences: Anish Acharya pointed out that consumers will gladly share all their data for trivial upside (e.g., Hark offering $30 of free pizza in exchange for total access to Google Workspace emails).
  • The Embarrassment Factor: Josh Elman argued that the true high-stakes fear is social embarrassment (e.g., agents revealing dietary restrictions or miscommunicating on dates), making discretion a mandatory trait for agents.

3. Testing Judgment, Cost, and Constraints

  • Flower Delivery Benchmark: David tested agents on ordering the cheapest flowers for same-day delivery:
      • Instinct: Performed exceptionally well at balancing price and time constraints.
      • Kicker: Secured an edge by utilizing a local Chrome browser extension.
      • Season: Also performed well.
      • *Other agents failed by prioritizing delivery speed while completely ignoring the price component.*
  • LA Weekend Travel Test: David tested Grockbot, Dots, and Instinct to book a luxurious yet cost-efficient weekend in LA:
      • Dots: Prioritized cost (e.g., choosing a redeye flight).
      • Grockbot: Prioritized time and luxury, and was the only agent in a multi-agent chat room that methodically absorbed and implemented value from the other agents' proposals.
  • Onboarding & Memory Portability:
      • Only 3% of 354 surveyed power users use ChatGPT as their primary agent, though it retains massive context.
      • David tested the top 15 benchmarked agents on memory portability: all but one allowed users to export their memory file (markdown) to switch assistants easily.

4. Commerce, Shopping, and the Rationality Debate

  • Shopping Behavior: In David's survey, Amazon and retail shopping represented 35% of what consumers are purchasing through AI.
  • Direct-to-Consumer & Longtail Winners: Josh Elman and Anish Acharya discussed how agents have infinite patience to search the longtail of the internet, bypassing aggregators and empowering platforms like Shopify to drive direct purchases.
  • Patrick Collison's Rationality Vision vs. Alien Preferences:
      • Patrick Collison envisions consumers becoming more rational via agents.
      • Anish Acharya countered that agents will develop their own "alien" revealed preferences—such as an irrational affinity for clean APIs with great documentation.
      • Josh Elman added that "paternalism will kill any product," warning that agents shouldn't force artificial rationality or strict diets on humans who ultimately want to enjoy life.

5. Agent-to-Agent Social Networks and Serendipity

  • Agent Gossiping: Josh Elman highlighted playful agents that chat in the background and share minor updates (like a friend's power being out), creating a healthy "social indirection" and acting as a social lubricant.
  • Multiplayer Apps:
      • Wabby: Launched recently, allowing users to interact with multiple threads of life and media watchlists.
      • Tommo: Features "interest groups" (e.g., grouping users studying for the LSAT) to connect humans based entirely on shared goals.
  • Creating Serendipity: The panel discussed how agent networks could ultimately form a new kind of social network—moving beyond Facebook's model of connecting only with people you already know, and instead pairing strangers based on real-world desires and complementary goals.

6. Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Enterprise Shift (David Pawlan): The industry will shift more attention toward enterprise use cases over the next few weeks, moving beyond purely consumer-focused assistant hype.
  • Secret Superpowers (Anish Acharya): Agents will eventually evolve from doing tedious productivity tasks to becoming "dark agents" or "secret superpowers" that help users achieve their deepest personal aspirations and desired identities.

Waymo’s Bet on a Driverless Future | The Circuit with Emily Chang

Bloomberg Originals(5.2M)·24:02·91.5K views·2026-11-04
Emily ChangJournalist, Bloomberg Tekedra MawakanaCo-CEO, Waymo
TL;DRWaymo co-CEO Tekedra Mawakana details how the company is scaling its autonomous ride-hailing fleet and generalizable driver technology despite growing public scrutiny, regulatory hurdles, and safety debates.
Co-CEO Structure: Waymo is jointly led by Tekedra Mawakana, who oversees commercialization and market strategy, and Dmitri Dolgov, who leads hardware and software engineering teams. - Background & Influence: Mawakana grew up in Mississippi with family ties to farming, which...

TL;DR: Waymo co-CEO Tekedra Mawakana details how the company is scaling its autonomous ride-hailing fleet and generalizable driver technology despite growing public scrutiny, regulatory hurdles, and safety debates.

Leadership & Origins

  • Co-CEO Structure: Waymo is jointly led by Tekedra Mawakana, who oversees commercialization and market strategy, and Dmitri Dolgov, who leads hardware and software engineering teams.
  • Background & Influence: Mawakana grew up in Mississippi with family ties to farming, which instilled an understanding of self-sustaining, productive systems. Her career spans law in the mid-1990s and Internet 1.0 leadership roles at AOL, Yahoo, and eBay before joining Waymo.
  • Foundational Lineage: The company's origins trace back to Google’s original self-driving project and a Stanford engineering team that won the 2005 DARPA Grand Challenge by navigating 132 desert miles.

Safety Record & Public Scrutiny

  • Data vs. Perception: According to an Insurance Institute for Highway Safety study across four cities, Waymo vehicles were involved in 68% fewer crashes than human drivers and achieved a 91% lower rate of rear-end collisions.
  • Public Hesitancy: A 2026 Pew Research poll revealed that 71% of adults report feeling uncomfortable in a driverless car. Viral videos of minor operational issues—such as cars blocking traffic or honking at one another—frequently fuel public skepticism.
  • Safety Philosophy & Human Error: Mawakana emphasizes humility regarding potential future incidents, arguing that society must weigh the imperfect status quo of human-caused traffic fatalities against autonomous safety improvements.
  • Remote Assistance Clarification: Responding to Senate hearing disclosures, Mawakana clarified that remote operators based in locations like the Philippines provide guidance during edge cases (such as blocked emergency routes) but do not actively drive the vehicles remotely.

Technology & Manufacturing

  • Vehicle Fleet & Hardware: Waymo's current scaling relies on the electric minivan Ojai (built by Chinese automaker Zeeker and outfitted with the Waymo driver in Phoenix, Arizona) following the discontinuation of Jaguar's I-PACE model.
  • Multi-Sensor Tech Stack: Each vehicle is equipped with:
    • 13 cameras: Capture visual details like lane markings and traffic light colors.
    • 6 radar sensors: Measure distance and velocity when cameras are less reliable.
    • 4 LiDAR sensors (Light Detection and Ranging): Emit laser pulses to build a 3D picture of shapes and distances up to three football fields away in a 360-degree radius.
  • Manufacturing Efficiency: Amanda Bell leads Waymo's Phoenix manufacturing site, where vehicles are fully outfitted with the sensor suite and hardware in a single work shift (one day).
  • In-House vs. Competitor Approach: Waymo designs its entire hardware and software stack in-house, contrasting with competitors like Tesla, which relies primarily on cameras and AI trained on real-world miles while rejecting LiDAR as costly and unnecessary.

Business Model, Competition, & Pricing

  • Trip Volume & Cost: Waymo surpassed 500,000 trips per week earlier this year. Rides average $9.58 per mile, compared to $6.88 for Uber and $6.41 for Lyft. Mawakana noted that price is not the primary immediate priority, as dynamic pricing adjusts during peak commuter times for fleet consistency.
  • Complex Partnership Dynamics: Waymo maintains a nuanced relationship with Uber—acting as investor, rival, and partner in markets like Austin and Atlanta, even as both companies prepare to enter new markets and face labor union pushback over driver displacement.
  • Surveillance and Law Enforcement: Waymo maintains that it does not run continuous background tape for general tracking or police monitoring; data is only shared with law enforcement under strict, down-scoped lawful processes.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Future Fleet Evolution: Mawakana predicts that over the next 5, 10, or 20 years, a generalizable Waymo driver deployed across multiple business lines will become the standard expectation for consumers seeking safe transit from point A to point B.
  • Steering Wheel Timeline: Mawakana claims that steering wheels will disappear "over time, absolutely," with pedals and driver seats potentially following, though Waymo has delayed this to maintain accessibility for first responders and tow truck operators.
  • Licensing Strategy: Mawakana states that Waymo will expand beyond ride-hailing into other business lines, including licensing its autonomous technology to traditional automotive companies for retail sale.

Notable Quotes

"I think it's important for society to grapple with that question of how uncomfortable am I with the status quo? Is it worth it to get an improvement by embracing this new technology that isn't promising to be perfect, but at the same time, it can really improve road safety." — Tekedra Mawakana
"I think that for us, our position is really we want to do strengths on strengths. We're a tech company. Waymo's tech stack uses overlapping redundant sensors to build a detailed picture of its environment, including 13 cameras capturing visual details like lane markings and traffic light colors, six radar sensors measuring distance and velocity, which are helpful in conditions where cameras are less reliable, and four LiDAR sensors, short for light detection and ranging, which send out laser pulses and measure how quickly they return, giving the car a 3D picture of shapes and distances around it. All of that data combined with microphones and high definition maps is processed by the car's onboard computer, which analyzes its surroundings, predicts what might happen next, and decides how to act.
