What Happens When the AI Boom Slows Down

Invest Like The Best(115.0K)·74:57·13.8K views·2026-11-04
Martin CasadoGeneral Partner, Andreessen Horowitz Patrick O'ShaughnessyHost, Invest Like The Best
TL;DRMartin Casado argues that AI represents an industrial revolution rather than a software cycle, transforming engineering constraints into capital allocation problems while exposing the limitations of traditional venture mindsets.
Four Investor Archetypes: Martin Casado identifies four distinct mindsets in private markets: Traditional Finance: Relies on SaaS metrics and market size as proxies for business health. Productive Investors: Focuses on technical approaches, adjacent markets, technology...

TL;DR

Martin Casado argues that AI represents an industrial revolution rather than a software cycle, transforming engineering constraints into capital allocation problems while exposing the limitations of traditional venture mindsets.

1. Investor Mindsets & Venture Capital Realities

  • Four Investor Archetypes: Martin Casado identifies four distinct mindsets in private markets:
    • Traditional Finance: Relies on SaaS metrics and market size as proxies for business health.
    • Productive Investors: Focuses on technical approaches, adjacent markets, technology evolution speed, and team execution.
    • Frontier Investors: Romanticized 1960s/70s style ("YOLO"), backing founders based purely on talent networks and visionary science fiction tropes.
    • Speculative Investors: Focuses exclusively on market timing rather than underlying asset growth.
  • Misapplied Financial Metrics: Applying late-stage or traditional SaaS financial mindsets (like low gross retention or high-margin expectations) to early-stage disruptive technology is self-defeating. Disruptive technology naturally lacks established business models or immediate TAM clarity.
  • Market Efficiency: Private markets are efficient; Casado rejects the idea of "truffle-hunting" non-consensus deals or beating the market through cleverness, viewing competitive rounds and market pricing as the norm.
  • The TAM Fallacy: Disagrees with zero-sum views (such as those held by Sequoia's Roloff) that venture capital is overfunded; capital creates opportunities and directly expands the investable TAM.

2. Infrastructure Refactoring & The Agent Era

  • Infrastructure Stack Rebuild: Infrastructure (compute, network, storage, databases, and models) is experiencing maximum disruption as it is refactored for AI and agents.
  • OLTP Databases & Networking:
    • Transaction databases (OLTP) are seeing net-new entrants because AI agents eliminate the need to train humans on new, non-standard database query languages.
    • High-performance GPU clusters require rigid performance constraints, driving optical networking innovations and new networking companies for the first time since Arista.
  • Software Volume Surge: Vibe-coding and AI agents mean significantly more software is being written than ever before, which requires underlying compute, storage, and database infrastructure.

3. Models, Frontiers, and the Economics of Compute

  • The 3-to-6 Month Model Lifespan: The lifespan of a frontier model is extremely short (3 to 6 months). If a frontier lab stumbles or slips by 3 months, it risks losing the entire market.
  • Competitive Equilibria in Token Usage: Even when tasks are heavily automated, startups in competitive equilibria will always pay 10 times more for the smartest frontier model rather than a cheaper, dumber model.
  • Capital and Token Factories: Neo-clouds function as token factories with heavy vertical integration. The real bottleneck for these providers is physical infrastructure, specifically power supply and hardware chips (GPUs).
  • Regulatory Pressures & Space: With over 500 proposed legislations attempting to stop data center building in the U.S. (including Texas), future compute infrastructure may increasingly move internationally or even to space.

4. Cybersecurity & Digital vs. Physical Scaling

  • Cybersecurity Evolution: While AI is driving a "breach apocalypse," it mirrors the chaotic early days of the internet (such as vulnerable Windows 95 installations). The good guys will continue outspending bad guys to deliver technical security solutions, resulting in software that is ultimately much more secure.
  • Digital vs. Physical Worlds: Digital problems can be solved by throwing capital at them via Reinforcement Learning (RL) environments. However, scaling curves and distributions for robotics, the physical universe, medicine, and science remain unpredictable and unproven.

5. Strategic M&A and Deal Dynamics

  • Strategic Exits: Casado has participated in massive strategic M&A deals (including VMware, Tabular, Cursor, and World Labs). Key drivers include deep personal alignment between buyers and founders, and presenting a clear base case plus an inductive step (buying an option on a massive market).
  • Deal Killers: Leaking deals to the press and treating strategic M&A like a poker game or a competitive auction destabilizes transactions. Buyers like Mark Zuckerberg or Lisa Su are deeply sophisticated and require trust, good faith, and professional alignment.

Notable Quotes

"I mean, let's be honest, software has sucked. Technology's been hard to use. Like, it's most often broken. And now we're actually in a point where this stuff could actually start working." — Martin Casado
"Homie, we didn't even know how to monetize the internet... like business models always lag disruptive technologies." — Martin Casado
"I'm more and more of the belief that this is an industrial revolution level shift. And for me the industrial revolution went from basically a zero sum world to like a... nonzero sum where value is being created net new value is being created." — Martin Casado
I mean, let's be honest, software has sucked. Technology's been hard to use. Like, it's most often broken. And now we're actually in a point where this stuff could actually start working. People keep thinking of these things as software, and it's not. And I think we just keep mapping it to things like search and it's not. Like, we're starting to realize how powerful this stuff is. And I just think that like the biggest constraint is no longer the tech. It's literally our creativity. You recently wrote about sort of four buckets of styles of investing that might be applied in call it venture, I don't know, big private market tech, let's call it. Can you enumerate those and just like say a sentence on on perspective of each? Yeah, yeah, yeah. Again, I feel like I'm just in this point in my career. I've been doing this for a decade. I've done hundreds of deals. And I sit in these discussions, and I just realize people aren't communicating very well. So I kind of did this exercise where I'm like, okay, so what are the mindsets people bring to investing? And I think there's basically four types of mindsets. So the one that we were talking about before is like this traditional finance mindset, which is the correct proxy for the health of a business is the market it's going into and its finances. That's what it is. Spreadsheet stuff. Yeah. It's like what are the SAS metrics? What is the size of the market? And that is a proxy for the entire business. So that's one. And clearly growth investors do this, but like more and more you see like early stage series A people do this right, which to me is a little bit discordant. Then there's what I call productive investors, which you believe you're investing in a productive asset, and the productive asset is what's creating value. And if you believe in that, as it creates value, it will create market, and as it creates value you're going to have to find ways for the business to catch up to that value. And so the type of diligence you do if you're a productive investor is more like what is the technical approach, what are the adjacent markets, how fast is this likely to evolve, how close is it to capturing value, like that sort of stuff. Is this the right team to build it? Then there's what I call frontier investing, which I think this is the romantic idealized version of VC from like the '60s and '70s, which is kind of yolo, man. It's like founders know better than VCs and they've seen something that I don't see and they're going to go out there and do it and like who am I to second guess them? And it's about... they almost look like talent networks, right? Like I would say Chris Dixon, remember when he was investing in nootropics, he invested in Soylent, he invested in Oculus, he invested in Coinbase, and in crypto. I mean, it's like every science fiction trope from the '70s and '80s you kind of invest in and you just don't overthink any of these things. So it's almost like the strong form of the productive. And then the last one, and it is always so surprising how prevalent it is, is speculative investors, which they don't even care about the asset. They just want to time a market, right? And so like you'll see this like in crypto and in secondary markets where they don't know about the underlying asset. They probably don't even know very much about the actual like business metrics, but they're like, "Well, this looks cheap. It's clearly going to go up in value. Oh, this is the top of the market, I'm going to get out." And so they're trying to time markets. They're actually just doing speculative. They're not really interested in will the business grow. And again, the problem is if you have all of these archetypes, you can justify any deal or not justify any deal. So if you don't know what your beliefs are, you'll always basically just kind of justify whatever personal bias you have. If you think about yourself as a productive investor focused on disrupt, I love this idea that disruptive technologies, you should not look at some of these traditional things that so many investors talk about. Talk about what you do look at when you look at so many companies. What is your method by which to assess whether or not someone might be creating a truly disruptive piece of technology, really a valuable asset? So I only do computer science investing and I only do infrastructure investing, and I think infrastructure as an industry has a certain shape. And so it's not like... you know, it's got certain buying behaviors, the technology looks a certain way, and the industry acts a certain way. And so what you're trying to do is you're trying to take a technical approach and you're trying to map it into that technical framework, right? Like this is kind of what we try and do. And the way you do that is, you know, there's a number of approaches. One of them you're like what are the leading companies doing internally, and this will kind of give you a glimpse on like maybe this is how platforms evolve, or perhaps the team that is doing this did it in another context and now they're actually bringing it to this context. I think it's one of these things where how much can you diligence without actually just using the numbers for a proxy? As far as you know, you can talk to customers, will they use this? You can see if there's like adjacent budgets that are growing that you could kind of argue will fragment to become this, or maybe you have early signs of willingness to pay, but they actually haven't figured out the business model. I always think like every tech wave... I've been in the industry for 30 years... every tech wave people handwring about margins. Everyone... I remember for the cloud, I remember for mobile, I remember for the internet, you know, clearly now people worry about margins. So maybe you're like there's a willingness to pay and there's a lot of interest, but like we haven't figured out the cogs yet in the actual viable business model. So like you're using partial indicators, not full indicators, in order to do this. And so my team... my team has a lot of technical people, product people, many have PhDs, and like, listen, we don't second-guess the tech. The founder will know the tech better than us. But we can ask the question, how does this tech fit into the broader platforms? How does the tech fit into spending? How does this tech fit in with our understanding of stickiness and defensibility? And then we look at early market numbers and we'll say, "Hey, listen, like a financial investor would puke at this, but it actually shows that the customers are really interested to figure something out, and will they be able to figure something out now?" So there's a lot of diligence you can do where with frontier investing, I don't think you do any of that. You're like, it's people. Is this the best team to do this crazy thing? And then you just YOLO? You were saying before we hit start that nobody understands network effects. Can you explain what you meant? I've realized that even the most common terms we use, there's no consensus on. And so, for example, network effects is the favorite one that I pick on. I asked you before... I messed it up. No, you didn't mess it up. You said what like a third of people say. So I said, so what is a network effect? What did you say? I said a product that gets better as it adds a marginal user. So to me, this is almost a tautological definition because most products will get better as you add a user, right? Like they will find a bug, they will provide feedback, they will tell other people, you know, and so that doesn't say anything about defensibility. But other people when they use the term network effects, they actually mean the original meaning of the term network effect from the '70s, which is a telephone network where when users join they interact with each other, that grows superlinearly, and that's why you get like natural monopolies. And other people use the term network effect more to mean like an actual flywheel. So like a self-reinforcing flywheel. So not just like things get better, but like it self-reinforces in some sort of a way. This is where you hear the term like data network effects. But even in that sense, you don't know if that self-reinforcement is superlinear or not. So you don't know if it makes it defensible or not. So you have this very common word that's on 70% of pitch decks where a founder is sitting in front doing a pitch and nobody has consensus on what it means. So how would you define it? So I think the root of the confusion is not a definitional problem. I think the root of the confusion is conceptually we conflate the mechanism with the shape of the moat. And let me explain. So there's many things that could create a moat, right? Like a brand scale, a two-sided marketplace, a network effect. But that moat, to reason about that, is only really useful if it has some properties that make the company defensible against somebody else, right? So if that moat grows superlinearly with scale like a classic Metcalfe's law network effect would, that's really defensible. If it grows sublinearly with scale, it may not be useful at all. And so I think you and I have a conversation of defensibility. I think the first thing we need to do is not conflate the shape of the moat with the mechanism that caused it. So first I think we should say the moat that we have is superlinear, it's linear, or it's sublinear with scale. And then the second thing, a lot of things actually contribute to a moat, right? To that. For example, we mentioned network effects or scale effect. What's a scale effect? The scale effect to me is as the companies scale, it can reduce unit economics, normally because it has pricing power for example or maybe CAC goes down because of brand. What's a two-sided marketplace? That's very different to me than a network effect. That's where basically you've got self-reinforcing supply and demand. So I think that there are many mechanisms and then there's the shape, and then to have a discussion I think you want to make sure that the shape is something that actually provides long-term defensibility. Then the last thing I'll say is it's almost impossible to have these conversations in isolation because we're actually talking about competitive equilibria. Because if you have a network effect, then maybe the competitor has a network effect too, and maybe if they have more capital than you and grow faster, that probably impacts the shape of your moat, right? Like you may not grow as fast marginally because they're stealing your customers or something like that. And so I just think that we need very, very basic concepts. Why Datasite Stopped Building Its Own Parsing - Adit Abraham, Reducto & Raj Bakhru, Datasite

AI Engineer(983.0K)·19:49·2.9K views·2026-11-04
Raj BakhruGlobal CTO, Datasite Adit AbrahamCo-founder and CEO, Reducto
TL;DRDatasite global CTO Raj Bakhru and Reducto CEO Adit Abraham discuss scaling AI across virtual data rooms handling 60% of $100M+ deals, explaining why outsourcing document parsing to Reducto allowed Datasite's 250 engineers to focus on high-value GraphRAG and agentic products.
Company Leadership: Raj Bakhru serves as Global CTO at Datasite; Adit Abraham is Co-founder and CEO at Reducto. - Transaction Volume: Datasite handles approximately 60% of all global corporate transactions over $100 million. - Platform Scale: - Over 500,000 monthly active...

TL;DR

Datasite global CTO Raj Bakhru and Reducto CEO Adit Abraham discuss scaling AI across virtual data rooms handling 60% of $100M+ deals, explaining why outsourcing document parsing to Reducto allowed Datasite's 250 engineers to focus on high-value GraphRAG and agentic products.

Background & Scale of Datasite

  • Company Leadership: Raj Bakhru serves as Global CTO at Datasite; Adit Abraham is Co-founder and CEO at Reducto.
  • Transaction Volume: Datasite handles approximately 60% of all global corporate transactions over $100 million.
  • Platform Scale:
    • Over 500,000 monthly active users accessing content.
    • Generates a trillion-plus tokens per year.
    • Processes 55,000 virtual data rooms (VDRs) a year.
    • Experienced 40% top-line growth over the 18 months following the BlueFlame acquisition.

The BlueFlame Acquisition Journey

  • Founding & Bootstrap: Raj Bakhru co-founded the vertical AI M&A startup BlueFlame after running M&A/strategy for a security firm he previously sold. BlueFlame bootstrapped initially to save costs.
  • The Acquisition: Approached Datasite for a $5 million investment round during VC fundraising, which resulted in Datasite acquiring the 50-person company instead over a dinner meeting.
  • Competitive Pressure: BlueFlame realized that competing against venture-backed vertical AI rivals (who raised hundreds of millions) and well-funded frontier model labs would require massive dilution, prompting the acquisition.
  • Proprietary Data Advantage: The acquisition combined BlueFlame with Datasite's VDR ecosystem and Grata (a private company database with 20 million companies), creating an isolated, highly differentiated proprietary data moat.

Parsing Challenges & The Build vs. Buy Strategy

  • The Endless Edge Cases: Scrapily built ingestion pipelines failed on complex financial documents. Edge cases included:
    • Pie charts failing because a legend's shade of blue didn't correlate perfectly with the LLM's interpretation.
    • Complex Excel spreadsheets featuring merged cells, XLSB macro-enabled files, and templates.
  • Engineering Allocation: Datasite employs 250 engineers divided across 5 major revenue-generating initiatives.
  • Outsourcing Logic: Bakhru determined that supporting document extraction mechanics was a solved problem not worth consuming valuable engineering bandwidth, leading Datasite to partner with Reducto.

Architecture & Evaluation (VDR Bench)

  • Data Ingestion Pipeline: Large bursts of transaction data (up to a terabyte dropped instantly by bankers) pass through Reducto within Datasite's secure environment.
  • Vector Storage & GraphRAG: Processed data feeds into Turbo Buffer as the vector database, powering GraphRAG queries executed via Python code.
  • VDR Bench: Datasite's internal evaluation benchmark consisting of hundreds of documents and hundreds of questions with known answers.
  • Evaluation Results: Upgrading parsing with Reducto improved VDR Bench recall by 22%, outperforming direct off-the-shelf frontier lab tools (such as Claude Projects or OpenAI Projects).

Notable Quotes

*"It's all edge cases. Like literally everything is an edge case... the devil's in the details on all of this stuff."* — Raj Bakhru
*"If I can outsource everything on the layers underneath that [core business logic], that's an ideal split of time for my team."* — Raj Bakhru
*"A lot of people think that sort of all the work needs to be done on the other side, the answering side, and most of the work actually is on the extraction side. You get that right, and everything else can follow."* — Raj Bakhru
